VOLUME ONE
 The Inheritance of Frequency

VOLUME ONE
 The Inheritance of Frequency

===== PAGE 9 =====
9
 PROLOGUE
 THE WATCHER'S GIFT. BOHEMIA, 1267
I wasn't there. Nobody alive was there. But the manuscripts survived, and the
monastery records survived, and the margin notes in Brother Aldric's own hand
survived seven centuries of weather and war and institutional forgetting. What follows
is my reconstruction, part scholarship, part imagination, part a knowing that happens
when you've held the pages a dead man copied and felt the weight of what he carried.
I've done my best to honor the evidence. Where the evidence runs out, I've followed the
patterns. They have never lied to me yet.
 - Blake Masters, Mýrdalsjökull, Iceland
Restless candlelight guttered.


===== PAGE 10 =====
MASTERS X
10
 Brother Aldric steadied his hand. Seven years. Two thousand, five
hundred and fifty-six days of transcription. And now, in the small hours
before Matins, each final figure.
 He dipped the quill in ink that had cost the monastery three
months' tithes. Lamp black mixed with iron gall, thickened with dark
arabic until it flowed like blood from a fresh wound. The parchment was
calf vellum, scraped so thin that candlelight passed through it like
stained glass. Forty calves had died for this manuscript. Aldric had
blessed each one.
 The proportional figure before him was the last of seven hundred
and twelve. A circle inscribed within a circle, bisected by lines that met at
angles his mathematics could not name. The original had been written
by hands not entirely human, and Aldric had long since stopped trying
to understand how such precision was possible.
 He set the quill to vellum one last time.
 The ink flowed. The circle closed. And for a moment the angular
pattern seemed to rotate through dimensions the candlelight couldn't
explain.
 Aldric blinked. The figure was still. Ink on vellum. Nothing more.
 And his hands would not stop shaking.
"You came." Aldric didn't turn from the manuscript. "I wondered if you
would."
 The hooded figure moved into the scriptorium without sound,
without disturbing the candle flame. It had entered through a door


===== PAGE 11 =====
HOLLOWAY
11
Aldric had bolted from the inside three hours ago.
 When the figure spoke, its words bypassed his ears entirely. They
arrived fully formed, like memories of conversations he had never had.
 Is it finished?
 "The transcription is complete." Aldric turned. The hood revealed
nothing, not darkness, not light, simply an absence where a face should
be. "But you knew that."
 We observe. We do not know. There is a difference.
 "Is there?" Aldric gestured at the manuscript. "You gave us this
knowledge. You must have known what we would do with it."
 We gave you potential. What you do with potential is what makes
you interesting.
 The figure moved closer. In seven years, Aldric had learned many
things about his visitor's kind. They did not harm. They did not help.
They watched with patience that made mountains seem impetuous.
 But he had also learned they could be surprised.
 "I divided it," he said.
 The figure went still. Stiller than stone, stiller than death.
 Explain.
 Aldric pointed to seven leather satchels arranged along the
scriptorium wall.
 "Seven parts. Seven locations. Prague, where the emperor collects
wonders he does not understand. Paris, where the university burns
books it cannot control. Rome, where the Holy Father keeps secrets
older than his faith. Constantinople, where the libraries still remember
Alexandria. Uppsala, where the old gods are not quite forgotten. Toledo,


===== PAGE 12 =====
MASTERS X
12
where the translators turn Arabic wisdom into Latin ignorance." He
paused. "And one location I will not name. Not even to you."
 You scatter what we gave you as one.
 "I protect what you gave us." Aldric's voice hardened. "Brother
Matthias used the figures last spring. He saw everything. Every pattern,
every connection. And it broke him. He clawed out his own eyes trying
to make the visions stop." His hands clenched. "I held him while he
died."
 Some minds are stronger.
 "And some are not. And you do not care which is which." Aldric
stepped closer than he had ever dared. "You watch. You find us
interesting. But you do not protect. That burden falls to us."
 They nearly destroyed themselves once. With fragments. What
makes you believe scattered pieces are safer than the whole?
 "Because the whole is too beautiful." His voice cracked on the
word. "The patterns, the geometries. They are the most beautiful things
I have ever seen. More beautiful than sunrise over the mountains. More
beautiful than my mother's face, which I can barely remember. More
beautiful than the face of God, which I have sought my entire life."
 He took a breath.
 "And humans cannot resist the beautiful. We will seek it out, piece
it together, no matter the cost. So I scattered the pieces far enough apart
that finding them all will take lifetimes. Centuries. And in that time,
perhaps we will become ready."
 Or perhaps you will simply delay the inevitable.
 "Perhaps." Aldric almost smiled. "But delay is all we have ever had.
Delay between birth and death. Between ignorance and wisdom.


===== PAGE 13 =====
HOLLOWAY
13
Between the question and the answer." He turned back to the original.
"This is my gift to the future. Not knowledge. Time."
The fire took the manuscript slowly.
 The flames licked at its edges, darkened its binding, curled its pages,
but the geometric figures at its heart seemed to repel the fire itself.
 Then the figures began to move.
 Rotating through angles that had no names. The circles spun. Here,
the triangles folded through themselves. The manuscript was dying, and
in its death it was revealing what it had always contained. Information
encoded in proportion, meaning stored in mathematical relationships
that transcended the medium that carried them.
 The whispers came then. Thousands of voices speaking in
mathematics, in patterns that burned themselves into Aldric's vision
even as he squeezed his eyes shut.
 They were beautiful. They were terrible. They were true.
 And then they were ash.
 Aldric stood trembling in the smoke. His copies. Seven imperfect
echoes. Were packed and sealed. Seven years of work. Each fragment
carrying a fraction of the whole. The system demanded unity. His
response had been to deny it that unity, to scatter the invitation across
the known world and trust that centuries of distance would serve as
quarantine until the species could survive the cure.
 The figure had not moved.
 Someone will reassemble it. Someone always does.


===== PAGE 14 =====
MASTERS X
14
 "Then we must pray they are ready." Aldric held out the seventh
satchel. "Take this one. Hide it where humans cannot go. Not yet."
 We do not interfere.
 "You gave us the original. That was interference enough."
 For a long moment, nothing moved. The candles burned. The
embers cooled. Somewhere in the monastery, a bell began to toll for
Matins.
 The figure reached out. Fingers that were not quite fingers closed
around the satchel. And then the figure was gone, and Aldric was alone
in the scriptorium with six remaining packages and a lifetime of
questions he would never answer.
He stood at the window until dawn.
 Snow had begun, fat flakes that caught the torchlight and turned the
world to shifting white. Each one a hexagonal geometry, a crystalline
proof that the patterns existed at every scale.
 Beyond the gates, wolves circled the treeline. Their eyes caught the
light. Amber points moving through the darkness with the patience of
creatures that understood time differently than monks did. They sensed
the geometry. Not as knowledge. As vibration.
 Seven pieces. Seven locations. Perhaps seven centuries before
someone found them all. The figures were too beautiful to remain
hidden forever. Beauty had a way of surfacing, no matter how deep you
buried it.


===== PAGE 15 =====
HOLLOWAY
15
 The question was whether that call would be answered by someone
who could survive it.
 "May they be worthy," he whispered. Not a prayer. A hope cast
forward through time, aimed at strangers who would not be born for
generations.
 The bell for Matins rang again. Bronze and old.
 Aldric gathered his cloak. Checked the first satchel. Prague. Hoisted
it onto his shoulder. The weight was less than he expected. Seven years
of transcription, and the physical result weighed less than a sack of grain.
 The knowledge had always been lighter than its consequences.
 He walked out into the snow and the dark and the howling of
wolves.
 Behind him, in the hollow scriptorium, the last candle guttered and
died.
758 years later.

===== PAGE 17 =====
17
 CHAPTER ONE
 THE LIBRARY BOY
 109 Hz · Kansas City
The corner table at Miller Nichols Library had been Blake Masters'
territory for three years.
 Back to the wall. Clear sightline to both exits. Close enough to the
radiator to stay warm in October, far enough to avoid the clanking when
the old pipes kicked on. His grandfather had taught him to choose seats
like this. Always know your exits. William Masters had flown
reconnaissance aircraft over hostile airspace for twelve years, and the
habits he'd brought home from the Cold War had filtered down through
two generations like table manners: automatic, inherited, instinct rather
than training.


===== PAGE 18 =====
MASTERS X
18
 Blake adjusted the Breitling Navitimer on his wrist and turned
another page of Foucault's Pendulum. Fourth time through. Some
obsessions weren't chosen. Some found you the way water finds cracks.
Patiently, inevitably, following the path of least resistance until
something gives.
 He didn't notice the woman at the table three rows over. Not yet.
 She was watching him.
The watch had been his grandfather's. A 1967 Breitling Navitimer, the
kind pilots wore when pilots still navigated by the stars. William Masters
had bought it the year he left Northrop, the year he stopped flying
experimental aircraft and moved his family from California to Missouri
for reasons nobody discussed.
 Blake's father had worn it next. James Masters, electrical engineer,
Missouri infrastructure division. He'd worn it until September 2010,
when he'd stopped wearing anything at all.
 Now Blake wore it. Too big for his wrist. Heavy with history.
 What had his grandfather been running from?
 Blake had spent five years of graduate school and one failed security
career trying to answer that. All he had were fragments: the watch, some
photographs, his father's memories of William taking him to tour
McDonnell Douglas, and a growing certainty that somewhere between
his grandfather's test flights and his father's nuclear security clearance,
the Masters family had stumbled across something that didn't want to be
found.


===== PAGE 19 =====
HOLLOWAY
19
 The woman three tables away shifted. Her eyes found his again,
held for one beat too long, then slid away.
 Blake felt the hair rise on the back of his neck.
She was good. Professional.
 Peripheral glances every thirty seconds, never holding eye contact
long enough to register as staring. Most people wouldn't have noticed.
 Auburn hair, cut practical and short. Green eyes that held
intelligence and something harder underneath. Caution, maybe. Or
ancient grief. She wore a black turtleneck and no jewelry except a silver
ring on her right hand. Her leather bag looked expensive but worn, the
kind that had crossed borders and survived searches.
 He cataloged all of this without appearing to look up from his
book. What do you see? Tell me everything about them without looking directly.
William's game.
 What Blake saw now was something he hadn't expected. A tension
in her shoulders that wasn't tactical. The way her fingers kept returning
to the silver ring, turning it. And her eyes, when they found his again
across thirty feet of library carpet, not just watching. Searching.
 Blake felt the prickling sensation at the base of his skull. The same
feeling he'd had at SubTropolis, right before they'd fired him. The same
one his father had described in his journal, the week before the accident
that wasn't an accident.
 Something was about to change.


===== PAGE 20 =====
MASTERS X
20
A cardinal landed on the windowsill.
 Male, brilliant red against the gray October afternoon. Blake stared
at it, and for a moment he was nine years old again, watching California
roll past beneath him at three thousand feet, his grandfather's hands
steady on the yoke.
 The summer of 1999. Blake's parents had wanted him to really
know his grandfather. They'd sent him to California for three months,
and William had picked him up at LAX with a hand-lettered sign that
said BLAKE and a grin that promised adventure.
 Their first flight had been over the coast. William had leveled off at
twenty-five hundred feet, the Pacific stretching to the horizon.
 "What do you see?"
 Blake, nine years old and pressed against the window, had squinted
at the white lines forming and reforming below. "Waves?"
 "What about the waves?"
 He'd looked harder. "They come in sets? Like one, two, three big
ones, then smaller ones."
 "Good. Now watch where they break. See how the wave height
changes with the bottom contour? Shallow water, the wave stands up.
Deep water, it rolls past. The ocean floor creates the pattern you see on
the surface."
 "Most people see the ocean and think it's chaos," William had said.
"But there's no chaos in nature, Blake. Only patterns we don't
understand yet."


===== PAGE 21 =====
HOLLOWAY
21
 The second flight had been different. Twenty minutes in, William
had said the words Blake had been hoping to hear:
 "Your airplane."
 Blake's hands found the yoke. Suddenly he could feel the aircraft.
Every shift in the air transmitted directly into his palms.
 "Gentle pressure. You're not wrestling it. You're asking it to dance."
 Blake nudged the yoke right and the world tilted. Mountains sliding
beneath them, the horizon canting, his stomach floating.
 "Now level it out. Feel when it wants to stop turning."
 And Blake had felt it. That moment when the forces balanced and
the world returned to horizontal. Not something William told him.
Something his hands knew.
 "You're a natural," William had said, and Blake had heard
something beyond pride. Recognition.
 They'd flown over the Mojave their third week. High desert,
forty-five hundred feet. William pointed down at the dry riverbeds
branching across the valley floor.
 "What do you see?"
 By now, Blake knew better than the obvious. "Arteries and veins.
All branching out."
 "The path of least resistance. But look closer. See how the angles
repeat? The same branching pattern at different scales?"
 Blake stared. The same angles, the same ratios, in channels a
hundred feet wide and in tiny erosion patterns barely visible from
altitude.


===== PAGE 22 =====
MASTERS X
22
 "A river does it," William continued. "Lightning does it. Blood
vessels in the hand achieve it. Trees do it. That's not just least resistance.
That's mathematics expressing itself through nature."
 "Why does nature keep using the same pattern?"
 "That is the question I've been asking my whole life."
 Something in William's voice had made Blake look over. His
grandfather was staring at the desert, but seeing something else entirely.
 "When I flew test planes," William said quietly, "I saw things.
Patterns in cloud formations that didn't match weather reports.
Geometric shapes in rock formations too precise to be natural. Once, at
fifty thousand feet, I watched a thunderstorm from above and saw..." He
paused. "Something I couldn't explain."
 "What was it?"
 "I don't know. I took photographs, filed reports. The engineers said
ice crystals. The meteorologists said lenticular clouds. But I knew what I
saw."
 Silence. Then: "Most people see the land and move on. But you.
You see the pattern and want to understand. That curiosity. That's the
beginning of wisdom."
 "Will I ever understand? All of it?"
 "No. Nobody understands all of it. But you'll see more than most.
You'll ask better questions." Quiet. "That's not always a blessing, Blake.
Sometimes the questions are heavier than the answers."
 Blake hadn't understood. Nine years old, flying over the desert with
his grandfather. He'd thought William meant academic questions.
 He hadn't known his grandfather was preparing him for something
else entirely.


===== PAGE 23 =====
HOLLOWAY
23
The cardinal flew away. Blake blinked, surfacing from twenty-six years
of memory.
 The woman with auburn hair was gone.
 She'd disappeared while he was three thousand feet above the
Mojave. Which meant she was better than good.
 She was very, very good.
The apartment was small. A studio in Westport, third floor walkup.
 Three photographs hung on the wall. William at Northrop, young
and confident, standing next to an aircraft blacked out with government
markers. James at Oscar-01, 2009, shaking hands with an Air Force
colonel. Blake himself in his G4S uniform, SubTropolis badge visible, a
thick black X drawn through it with permanent marker.
 Three generations of men who'd worked in places with secrets.
Two of them dead.
 Blake looked at the middle photograph the longest. His father's face
in that picture was the public face, the handshake, the uniform collar, the
professional smile aimed at someone holding a camera. But Blake
remembered a different face. The one James wore on the back deck of
the Jefferson City house on Thursday evenings, when he'd set up the
soldering station on the picnic table and build shortwave radios from
surplus parts.


===== PAGE 24 =====
MASTERS X
24
 Blake was thirteen. He'd come outside with his geometry
homework and a glass of the terrible lemonade his mother made from
powder because she refused to pay four dollars for lemons. James had a
circuit board clamped in a vise, a thin curl of solder smoke rising
between them.
 "You're making that look harder than it is," James had said without
looking up.
 "It's proofs. They want me to prove a triangle is a triangle."
 "That's not what they want. They want you to show your reasoning.
Proofs aren't about the answer. They're about the path."
 James had set down the iron. He smelled the way he always smelled
on Thursday evenings, solder flux, coffee, the cedar soap he used
because Lorraine said the regular soap gave him a rash. The specific
combination of a specific man on a specific evening, unreproducible, the
kind of detail that disappeared from the world when the person did and
could never be recovered from photographs or letters or the careful
language of obituaries.
 "Here." James had taken the pencil. Not to solve it, to draw a radio
schematic beside the triangle. "You know what a circuit is?"
 "Electricity going in a loop."
 "Electricity following a path. The path is the proof. You don't
prove the triangle exists. You prove why it has to exist. The angles don't
just add up to 180 degrees. They can't not." He'd tapped the schematic.
"Same with a circuit. Current doesn't choose to flow. The path makes it
inevitable."
 He'd grinned, the private grin, the one that wasn't for cameras. The
one that made the skin around his eyes crease in a way that said he was


===== PAGE 25 =====
HOLLOWAY
25
delighted by the elegance of something, and that the delight was a gift he
wanted to hand to his son the way you hand someone a glass of water.
Here. This is good. Drink.
 Blake had finished the proofs in twenty minutes. James had finished
the radio. They'd sat on the deck after dark, tuning through shortwave
frequencies, hearing weather reports from ships in the Gulf of Mexico
and ham operators in Manitoba and, once, a burst of numbers in
Russian that made James go quiet for a long time.
 "Dad?"
 "Just listening." James's hand on the dial, steady. "Some signals, you
pick up by accident. Some are aimed at you." He'd looked at Blake with
an expression that wasn't the grin anymore. Something older and sadder.
"The trick is knowing the difference."
 Blake hadn't understood. Thirteen years old, lemonade from
powder, the cicadas starting in the hackberry tree. He'd thought his
father was talking about radio.
 The photograph on the wall showed none of this. It showed the
handshake. The uniform. The professional distance that official
photography required. It didn't show the solder smoke or the crease
around his eyes or the particular way he'd handed Blake the pencil,
handle first, the way you hand someone a knife, which was the way
James did everything: considering the other person's grip before his
own.
 A wooden box sat on the shelf below. Oak, brass clasp oxidized to
green. Inside: a copy of Don Quixote his grandfather had given him at
twelve, the margins dense with handwritten notes Blake had never read
carefully.


===== PAGE 26 =====
MASTERS X
26
 The phone rang.
 Blake let it ring. He was thinking about the SD card hidden behind
his medicine cabinet mirror. Thirty-seven photographs of geometric
carvings in a section of SubTropolis that didn't appear on any official
map. The section he'd been fired for entering.
 The carvings shouldn't have existed. SubTropolis was a limestone
mine converted to underground storage. The main facility dated to the
1960s. But the tunnel Blake had found went deeper. Into bedrock that
predated the limestone. Into rock carved with patterns that made his
eyes water when he looked at them too long. The same branching angles
from William's Cessna. The same proportions, a hundred feet
underground.
 The same patterns. Different altitude.
 The phone stopped ringing. Then started again.
 Blake picked up.
"Mr. Blake Masters?" A woman's voice, professional. "Patricia
Thornton, attorney with Pemberton and Associates in Jefferson City."
 Jefferson City. His father's hometown.
 "This concerns your grandfather's estate. There's been a
development."
 "My grandfather died in 2003. His estate was settled years ago."
 "First National Bank recently underwent renovations. They
discovered a safety deposit box not in their main system. Established in
1968. Rental paid through 2025. Fifty-seven years in advance."


===== PAGE 27 =====
HOLLOWAY
27
 Blake's hand tightened on the phone.
 "The box lists only one authorized heir. Not your father. Not your
mother. You."
 1968. The year William stopped flying test planes and moved his
family to Missouri and never explained why. He'd paid for a box
fifty-seven years in advance, timed to arrive at just this moment.
 Before Blake was delivered. Before his father was born. William had
set this in motion, aimed at a grandson who did not yet exist.
 Which meant William had known. About the patterns. About
everything that would happen to James, and everything that would
happen to Blake.
 "Mr. Masters? Are you still there?"
 "Tomorrow," Blake said. "I'll be there tomorrow."
He didn't sleep.
 Blake sat in the dark, looking at the photographs. William. James.
Himself. Three men with the same eyes, the same jaw, the same curse of
seeing things others walked past.
 The third was about to open a box that had been waiting since
before he was born.
 Geometric motifs swam behind his closed eyes. The SubTropolis
photographs. Circles within circles, triangles at impossible angles.
Branching patterns like rivers, like lightning, like the Mojave washes
from three thousand feet.


===== PAGE 28 =====
MASTERS X
28
 He'd spent four years telling himself they were erosion, water
damage, the mind finding signal in noise. The same way his father had
probably written off the anomalies at Oscar-01, the same way William
had probably told himself what he saw from the cockpit were optical
illusions.
 But they weren't noise. They weren't illusions.
 They were beautiful. And terrible. And deliberately carved by hands
that understood something about geometry that modern mathematics
had forgotten.
 The question isn't whether they're there. The question is whether you're ready to
see them.
 Tomorrow. He would drive to Jefferson City. Open his
grandfather's safety deposit box. Start finding answers to questions that
had killed his father and driven his grandfather from the sky.
 The Breitling caught the streetlight. A pilot's watch on a pilot's
grandson, still ticking forward toward whatever William had planned for
this moment.
 Blake stood and walked to the window.
 Whatever altitude they appeared at.


===== PAGE 29 =====
29
 CHAPTER TWO
 SISTER NADIA
 109 Hz · Kansas City
The campus coffee shop occupied a converted Victorian house at the
edge of the UMKC quad.
 He'd chosen his usual booth. Wall behind him, clear sightline to the
door. Old habits. The same positioning that had made Sarah call him
exhausting before she'd left six months ago with a speech about how she
couldn't live with someone who treated every coffee shop like a
potential ambush site.
 She'd been wrong about the paranoia being irrational.
 She'd just been right that it was exhausting.


===== PAGE 30 =====
MASTERS X
30
 She moved too quietly. The floorboards should have announced
her. But the creaking came a half-second late, as if the wood itself had
been surprised to find her already inside.
 Auburn hair. Green eyes. The same woman from the library
yesterday, the one who'd watched him with professional patience and
disappeared without him seeing her leave.
 Closer now, she looked different. Younger. The intelligence was
still there, the caution, the old grief. But there was something else. A
restlessness, a coiled energy that wasn't quite anxiety. The energy of
someone who'd been waiting for something a long time and could feel it
arriving.
 "You're very careful about where you sit," she said, and sat down
across from him without waiting for an invitation.
 Blake didn't reach for his coffee. "Most people knock."
 "I'm not most people." She set her leather bag on the seat beside
her. Up close, the stitching had been repaired at one corner with thread
that didn't quite match. A bag that had been loved, not just used. "Sister
Nadia Volkov. I'm researching medieval manuscripts at the university."
 She flagged the barista without looking. Raised two fingers. Tea
arrived two minutes later, loose leaf, something herbal that smelled like
chamomile and something sharper underneath. Not coffee. Tea said
patience. Ritual. The kind of person who'd learned to wait for things to
steep.
 "Sister?" Blake glanced at her clothes. Black turtleneck, dark jeans,
silver ring on her right hand. No habit. No cross.
 "The order is informal. I took vows, Mr. Masters." A slight smile,
sad around the edges. "I just don't advertise them."


===== PAGE 31 =====
HOLLOWAY
31
 "You know my name."
 "I know many things." She studied him with eyes that held too
much knowledge for someone who looked barely thirty. "Your academic
work on medieval grimoires. Your time at SubTropolis. What happened
to your security clearance." She tilted her head. "I know your
grandfather was more than a pilot. And I know you already suspect
that."
 Blake's hand found his coffee cup. Not to drink. His throat had
gone too tight. But to hold something steady. She'd compressed what
she knew into implications rather than recitation, and that was more
unsettling than a full dossier. It meant she understood which details
mattered.
 "Whatever's in that safety deposit box," Nadia said, leaning
forward, "it's not family mementos. And the people who killed your
father would very much like to know what's inside."
 The words hit Blake like a physical blow. The people who killed your
father.
 "My father died in a car accident."
 "Your father died on a stretch of road he'd driven a thousand times,
in perfect weather, at two in the afternoon. Three days after he started
asking questions about anomalies at Oscar-01. Three days after he filed a
FOIA request for construction documents classified above his clearance
level."
 She pulled a slim folder from her bag, slid it across the table.
 "No skid marks. No mechanical failure. No witnesses. The official
report called it 'driver error.' A man with twenty years of perfect driving
losing control on a straight road in broad daylight." She leaned back.


===== PAGE 32 =====
MASTERS X
32
"Do you believe that, Mr. Masters?"
 Blake looked at the folder. Looked at Nadia. Her green eyes were
steady but not cold. There was something underneath. Pain, the kind
you learn to navigate around but never quite through. Her hands on the
table weren't entirely still.
 She was afraid of something too.
 "No," Blake said quietly. "I don't believe that."
 Nadia nodded. Not triumph. Relief. As if she'd been carrying this
knowledge alone for a long time and was genuinely grateful to share the
weight.
 "Good," she said. "Then we have something to talk about."
The model aircraft hung from William's garage ceiling on fishing line so
fine they seemed to float.
 SR-71 Blackbird. U-2 Dragon Lady. P-38 Lightning. A-12 Oxcart.
Blake had helped build each one over multiple summers, following
William's instructions about angles and proportions that mattered more
than they should for model airplanes in a garage.
 But William had been precise about everything. The angle of each
aircraft relative to the others. Here, the direction they faced. The height
at which they hung. He'd used a protractor and a level and strings to
ensure every measurement was exact.
 "Why does it matter where they point?" Blake had asked. Eleven or
twelve, that last summer before everything changed.


===== PAGE 33 =====
HOLLOWAY
33
 William had stood on a stepladder, adjusting the P-38's angle.
"Hidden in plain sight. The best secrets are the ones people see every
day without understanding."
 "Is there a treasure map on your ceiling?"
 William had laughed. The sound stuck in Blake's memory like a
splinter, sharp and precious. "Maybe. Would you know how to read it?"
 Blake had spent the rest of that summer studying the aircraft. The
SR-71 pointed northwest. The U-2 northeast. The P-38 southwest. The
A-12 southeast. Perfect cardinal directions, or close enough that the
deviation seemed intentional.
 He'd given up after three weeks. William had watched him quit with
sadness mixed with patience. The look of a man who knew his grandson
wasn't ready yet.
 Now Blake wondered if he was finally ready to read the map.
"The safety deposit box." Blake's voice was steadier than he felt. "How
do you know about that?"
 "I know you're planning to drive to Jefferson City tomorrow. And I
know you won't be the only one interested in what your grandfather left
behind."
 She reached into her bag and produced a plain white card. No logo.
Just a phone number in black type, and below it, two words: WHEN
READY.
 "Whatever's in that box, don't tell anyone you found it," Nadia said.
"And don't go alone."


===== PAGE 34 =====
MASTERS X
34
 "Why should I trust you?"
 "You shouldn't." Her smile again, not performed. Lived-in. "Trust
no one. Question everything. Verify before you believe. It's the only
thing that's kept your family alive this long."
 She walked away without looking back. Her footsteps made no
sound on the floorboards that had announced Blake like a herald and let
her pass like a ghost.
 Blake sat in the booth, holding the card, trying to understand why
he felt seen. For the first time in years, someone had looked at him and
recognized what they were looking at.
Her paper trail was perfect. Too perfect.
 Blake spent four hours hunting through databases and archives,
building a picture of Sister Nadia Volkov that should have reassured him
and instead made his skin prickle with the awareness that everything he
was finding had been designed to be found.
 Born 1994, Lviv, Ukraine. Orphaned at six. Raised by the Sisters of
the Holy Mother, a convent outside Kyiv. Immigrated at sixteen. Full
scholarship to Georgetown. PhD in Medieval History at twenty-five.
 Her dissertation: "Cognitive Transformation in Medieval
Grimoires: The Notory Arts Reconsidered." The thesis argued that
medieval grimoires were cognitive technologies. Systematic attempts to
enhance human perception through geometric visualization.
 She'd researched exactly what Blake had experienced in the tunnels
beneath SubTropolis. The patterns that had made his vision swim and


===== PAGE 35 =====
HOLLOWAY
35
his thoughts accelerate.
 Every detail confirmed. Every fact checkable. Perfect the way a

 But he couldn't stop thinking about her smile. The lived-in sadness
of it. The calloused fingertips. The repaired bag stitching. Small details
that didn't fit a manufactured identity.
 Either Sister Nadia Volkov was telling the truth, or she was very,
very good at lying.
 Blake looked at the card. WHEN READY.
 Was he ready?
He left before dawn. The Missouri River Runner. Blake needed to be
early, needed to walk the platform and watch who else was traveling

 The gray sedan was parked outside the station. Same car from
yesterday, same tinted windows. They weren't trying to hide.
 The train followed the river. October morning. Harvested corn

more vacant storefronts than open businesses.
 Missouri in autumn. Luminous and dying.
 He thought about Nadia's dissertation. About cognitive

The way she'd said your grandfather with something closer to reverence


===== PAGE 36 =====

than clinical interest.
 Don't go alone, she'd said.
 But Blake was alone. Had always been. Isolated by questions he

 Except they weren't coincidences.
 The Missouri State Capitol dome appeared through the window.
Jefferson City. A gray sedan idled in the station lot, different plates, same
make, same tinted windows.
 They'd been waiting for him.
 Blake walked to First National Bank, carrying his grandfather's
watch on his wrist and his father's questions in his head.
 And Nadia's card in his pocket, warm from being held too long.

 CHAPTER THREE
 THE BOX
 109 Hz · Kansas City
Blake followed Patricia Thornton down a hallway marked PRIVATE.
His hands were steady. The rest of him was not.
 "Box 1847," Patricia said, unlocking a heavy door marked VAULT
with a key and a code. "Established in 1968. Rental paid through
December 2025, fifty-seven years in advance." She led him into a room
lined with brass doors, the air cool and dry. "It would have lapsed in two
months. But your grandfather was very specific in his instructions. Only
one authorized heir."
 She pulled a card from her pocket. The handwriting was instantly
recognizable. William's precise script, the same hand that had filled the


===== PAGE 38 =====

margins of the Don Quixote Blake still kept on his nightstand.
 For Blake Masters. When he is ready.
 His hands started shaking.
 Patricia inserted two keys and turned them at once. The door

 A metal box, approximately twelve by eighteen by six inches deep.
Patricia lifted it out, set it on the table.
 "I'll give you privacy," she said. "When you're finished, press the
call button."
 She left. The vault door closed behind her with a sound like a
period at the end of a sentence.
 Blake stood alone with his grandfather's secrets.
 He opened the box.
Seven notebooks.
 Black leather binding, aged to a deep brown almost approaching
black. William's handwriting on the first page:
 Documentation. 1968-1998. WCM.
 Thirty years of documentation.
 Blake lifted the first notebook carefully. The leather was soft, oiled
over decades. He opened to a random page and saw geometric figures.
Circles inscribed within circles, triangles nested at precise angles, lines
extending into margins and wrapping around text in Latin and Greek
and languages Blake didn't recognize.


===== PAGE 39 =====
HOLLOWAY
39
 His eyes started to water. Not from emotion. From the patterns.
They pulled at his vision the way certain resonances pull at the inner ear,
made something deep in his neural architecture try to synchronize with
something it wasn't designed for. The same sensation he'd felt in
SubTropolis, amplified. Focused. Intentional.
 William hadn't just documented motifs. He'd been working with
them.
 Blake closed the notebook. Breathed.
 Six more underneath. He didn't open them. Not here. His hands
were shaking worse and his vision had gone soft at the edges, his brain
straining toward a vibration it could almost reach.
 Beneath the notebooks: a brass equipment tag. Engraved letters:
CR-60 / 68093. On the back: Oscar-01.
 His father's facility. The launch control center where James Masters
had worked, where he'd had clearance to go places civilians weren't
allowed, where he'd started asking questions and died three days later on
a road he'd driven a thousand times.
 A manila envelope, sealed with red wax that had cracked with age.
Written in William's hand:
 For Blake, when he's ready. Not before.
 Blake picked it up. Felt the weight of paper inside. Didn't open it.
 At the bottom: a black and white photograph.
 William Masters, young, maybe thirty, standing next to a Lockheed
U-2. The spy plane that flew at seventy thousand feet, so high the

 William wore a flight suit. He was smiling. Written on the back in

===== PAGE 40 =====

 First flight. 1956. I saw things from up there. -W
 Blake stared at his grandfather's young face. At the smile that would

 "What did you see up there?" Blake whispered. The vault's silence
absorbed the question without answering.
 He pressed the call button.
The return train wasn't until 2:15. Four hours to fill in a town that held
more history than he wanted to carry.
 Blake walked. The canvas bag bumped against his hip with every

Counter seats, black coffee for his father, hot chocolate for Blake, the

 He thought about calling Nadia.


===== PAGE 41 =====

 His thumb hovered over the call button.
 He put the phone away.
 Habit. The habit of carrying things alone, of being the person who

 Blake found a bench near the Capitol building. The Missouri River

 Inside: a single sheet of paper, handwritten. William's script, smaller

 Blake,
 If you're reading this, I'm gone. Your father is probably gone too. I've tried to
protect him, but the patterns don't stop once you start seeing them. They pull you
deeper. They consume you. They take everything.
 I saw them first from seventy thousand feet. U-2 reconnaissance, 1956. The
mission was classified, photographing Soviet installations. But I saw something else.
Patterns in cloud formations that matched mathematical sequences I'd seen in
medieval manuscripts. Proportional relationships in view features that shouldn't exist.
The higher I flew, the more I could see. And once I saw them, I couldn't unsee them.


===== PAGE 42 =====
MASTERS X

 I spent thirty years documenting what I found. The notebooks will show you.
The patterns exist at every scale, from quantum to cosmic. They're in cave paintings
from 30,000 BC. They're in medieval grimoires. They're carved into bedrock beneath

people like us, can perceive them.
 Your father has the gift. You have it too. I've watched you since you were nine

 But the gift comes with a price. You know that already, don't you? Your brain

 I think the symptoms are not disorder, they're adaptation. Your brain is trying

isolates you. It takes everything normal away.

 The seven notebooks document everything I learned. Read them carefully. Trust
what you see, even when people tell you you're imagining things. Trust your brain,
even when it betrays you.
 And when you're ready, when you've seen enough to understand, find the others.
They've been waiting.
 I'm sorry I couldn't tell you this while I was alive. I'm sorry I left you alone with
this. But you're not alone, Blake. You never were.
 Trust the patterns. Trust yourself.

 P.S. The model airplanes in my garage. They're a map. You'll understand when

 His hands had stopped shaking.

 He reached for his phone again. Nadia's number on the screen.

 CHAPTER FOUR
 THE ACCIDENT
 109 Hz · Kansas City
Two days since Jefferson City.
 The notebooks were in his apartment now. Seven of them, locked
in the fireproof safe he'd bought at Home Depot the morning after. He
hadn't read them yet, not really. He'd managed a few pages of the first
before closing it, hands shaking, head pounding, something behind his
eyes straining toward a vibration it couldn't quite reach.
 Whatever his grandfather had spent thirty years documenting,
Blake's brain wasn't ready for it.


===== PAGE 46 =====
MASTERS X
46
 But it couldn't leave them alone either. He dreamed about the
mathematical figures. Circles and triangles rotating through dimensions
that had no names. He'd wake at 3:47, as always, and sit at the kitchen
table staring at the safe in the closet, feeling the notebooks like a low
hum at the base of his skull.
 So here he was, back at Miller Nichols Library, pretending to work
on his dissertation while his mind circled the contents of that safe. The
notebooks had mentioned something. The Moreau collection. William
had visited this library in 1967, 1972, and 1985. Had made notes about
manuscripts hidden in plain sight where Blake had spent five years of
graduate school and never known to look.
 Blake closed his laptop. Walked toward the stacks.
The Miller Nichols Library had secrets.
 Not official ones, no locked cages, no forbidden sections. But Blake
had worked security long enough to recognize things hidden in plain
sight. A shelf unit slightly out of alignment. Catalog entries that didn't
match the physical collection. A door marked MECHANICAL that was
too clean to house machinery.
 He started in Medieval History, where he'd spent most of his
graduate career. But William's notebooks hadn't mentioned the canon.
They'd mentioned something older. Something that shouldn't exist in a
university library in Kansas City.
 Blake moved deeper. Medieval History gave way to Religious
Studies. Religious Studies to Comparative Religion. And Comparative


===== PAGE 47 =====
HOLLOWAY
47
Religion gave way to something the catalog called "Special Collections
(Uncataloged)", a single shelf unit against a back wall where the
fluorescent lights flickered and the air smelled like dust and forgetting.
 And at the top of the shelf, nearly hidden behind a row of bound
journals from the 1890s, a book with no title at all.
 Dark leather, worn smooth by centuries of hands. No text on the
spine. No library markings. No call number. Nothing that indicated this
book belonged to any official collection.
 Blake reached for the rolling ladder. Oak and brass, solid. He'd
climbed it dozens of times. Tested the first rung. Climbed.
 Three rungs up, his fingers just brushing the dark leather binding,
the rung beneath his foot gave way.
 Not cracked. Not broken. Just absent. As if the wood had decided
to stop being solid.
 Blake grabbed for the shelf. Missed. His shoulder struck wood. His
hip caught a corner. And then his head met the tile floor, and the world
went white.
Not black, white.
 Blake was aware of lying on his back. Cold tile beneath him. Pain
radiating everywhere. But the pain was distant, his nervous system
overwhelmed by input independent of the physical world.
 The white light was full of shapes.
 Proportional figures. Circles inscribed within circles, triangles
nested at precise angles, rotating through dimensions his eyes couldn't


===== PAGE 48 =====

track but his brain somehow followed.
 He knew these shapes. Had seen them in his grandfather's
notebooks. In the carvings beneath SubTropolis. In the flowstone at

years ago.
 The shapes pulsed. Once. Twice. In rhythm with something deeper
than heartbeat, some fundamental frequency beneath the waking world.
 See, something said. Not a voice. Information transmitted directly,
bypassing his ears.
 See.
 The white began to fade. Cognition reassembled itself in fragments.
The floor, the fluorescent lights, the smell of old books and his own
blood where he'd bitten his tongue.
 Everything too sharp, too detailed, edges too crisp, as if someone
had adjusted the resolution of reality.
 Something heavy pressed against his chest.
 The book. The one with no title. It had fallen with him.
 Blake opened the cover. No title page. Just pages covered in spatial
arrangements. Circles within circles, triangles within triangles. The
patterns moved. Or seemed to. Ink on vellum. But for a moment he
could have sworn he'd seen them rotate.
 He closed the book. Breathed.


===== PAGE 49 =====
HOLLOWAY

The cave was cold and the flashlight beam caught dust motes that hung

 Blake was ten years old. His grandfather stood beside him in the

 Meramec Caverns. Tourist attraction outside Sullivan, Missouri. But

 "See the formations?" William's voice echoed off walls that had

 Blake saw rock. Stalactites dripping from the ceiling. Stalagmites
rising to meet them. "They're just shapes," he said.
 "Nothing in nature is random, Blake." William crouched, eyes level
with his grandson's. He traced a finger along the stone curtain, following
lines Blake couldn't see.
 "It's the same pattern as the water that carved it. The same as the
streams on the surface. The same as the rivers." His eyes gleamed. "And
that structure is the same as the veins in your body. The same as the
branches of a tree. The same as lightning."
 "Goes all the way up?"
 "All the way down, too." William smiled. "Motifs repeat at every
scale. Quantum to cosmic. Everything connects."
 He stood, turning the flashlight toward deeper darkness.
 "Most people never see it," he said quietly. "They walk through a
world made of pattern and beauty, and they see chaos." He looked down

 "How will I know when I'm ready?"

He was on the floor and the floor was made of. Something. Something
that had a name and the name was. Linoleum. The name was linoleum

 Blake came back to himself on the library floor. The fluorescent

from the building's electrical system, from the HVAC, from the

 CHAPTER FIVE
 THE REVELATION
 109 Hz · Kansas City
Nadia knocked at 7:15 PM.
 Blake had spent the hour trying to clean up, not the apartment,
which was beyond help, but himself. Washed the blood from his hair.
Changed his shirt. Pressed a cold washcloth against the cut until the
swelling went down enough that he didn't look like he'd been in a bar

 He still looked like he'd been in a bar fight.
 When he opened the door, Nadia stood in the hallway holding two

 "Good," she said quietly, and pressed a butterfly bandage across the

 She looked up. Their faces were close.
 "Your brain is capable of far more than you normally access," she

Pre-Columbian. Pre-European. Pre-Christian."
 "The patterns were here first," Blake said slowly. "Everything in
medieval Europe was copied from North American sources."


===== PAGE 60 =====
MASTERS X
60
 "That's what your grandfather believed. What thirty years of
documentation supports." Nadia's eyes were bright. This wasn't the
operative or the nun. This was the scholar. "If we can prove it, we could
rewrite the history of human awareness."
 "And the Custodians want to stop that."
 "The Custodians killed your father to stop that."
 The words hung between them. Nadia held his gaze. Her hand on
the book had gone still, her ring finger pressing against the silver band.
 "I'm sorry," she said. "I know that's -"
 "Don't. Don't apologize for telling me what I already know."
 Silence. Two people sitting on a kitchen floor with a medieval
manuscript between them and a dead man's questions filling the space.
 Then Nadia laughed.
 Not at him. At the situation. At the two of them cross-legged with
pad Thai containers and antiseptic wipes and a book that shouldn't exist.
 It was the first time Blake had heard her laugh. It changed her entire
face, broke the composure, revealed something younger underneath.
 "What?" Blake said.
 "Nothing. It's just. I've been preparing for this conversation for two
years. I had it planned. Professional. Controlled." She shook her head.
"Not on a kitchen floor."
 "You wanted a PowerPoint presentation."
 "I wanted to seem competent."
 "You showed up with Thai food and bandages. That's competent
enough."


===== PAGE 61 =====
HOLLOWAY
61
 She smiled. Not the sad, knowing smile from the coffee shop.
Something warmer. Something that reached her eyes.
 "There's more to show you," she said. "A place. Our operational
base. But it can wait until tomorrow."
 "Why tomorrow?"
 "Because you have a concussion. Because you haven't slept.
Because -" She hesitated. "Because I'd rather stay here tonight. If that's. I
can sleep on the couch. I just don't think you should be alone right
now."
 It was a reasonable thing to say. Clinical, even. But Nadia's hand
was still on the book between them, and her fingers were two inches
from Blake's knee, and the way she'd said I'd rather stay carried a weight
apart from concussion protocol.
 "The couch is covered in manuscripts," Blake said.
 "I'll move them."
 "Blake." She looked at him steadily. "I'm offering to stay because
you shouldn't be alone. That's all."
 "I know."
 "Good." She stood. Began clearing the Thai containers. "Show me
which manuscripts I'm allowed to move."
Blake didn't sleep. Not really. He lay in the dark, the ceiling patterns
moving above him, and listened to Nadia breathing in the next room.


===== PAGE 62 =====
MASTERS X
62
 She'd cleared a space on the couch with the efficiency of someone
used to making order from chaos. Had arranged manuscripts into
careful stacks, handling them with the same reverence she'd given the
untitled book.
 Now she was asleep, or pretending to be, and Blake lay in the dark
and thought about what she'd told him. Keepers. Custodians.
Completion Sect. Three organizations fighting over ancient knowledge.
His grandfather had found the evidence. His father had died pursuing it.
 But that wasn't what kept him awake.
 What kept him awake was the laugh. The startled sound of Nadia
laughing on his kitchen floor. The way she'd said I wanted to seem competent
with a self-awareness that cut through every layer of craft and left
something honest standing in the kitchen light.
 At 3:47, Blake's eyes opened. He lay still, listening. Nadia's
breathing from the couch. The refrigerator hum. The motifs on the
ceiling, visible even without light.
 He got up. Went to the kitchen.
 She was already awake.
 "Can't sleep either?" Soft. Not startled. She'd heard him get up,
both of them tuned to frequencies most people couldn't hear.
 "3:47," Blake said. "Every morning."
 "William mentioned that in Notebook 3. A consistent wake time
associated with the integration process." A pause. "Two cups?"
 Blake made two cups. Brought one to the couch. Nadia sat up, the
blanket around her shoulders, her hair loose for the first time. She'd
worn it pulled back every time he'd seen her. Loose, it fell past her
shoulders, dark in the pre-dawn kitchen.


===== PAGE 63 =====
HOLLOWAY
63
 She took the cup. Their fingers brushed on the ceramic.
 "Tomorrow," Nadia said. "I'll take you to Genessee Street. The
operational base. There's more to show you. Maps, connections, your
grandfather's full research. And we need to talk about what comes next."
 "What comes next?"
 She turned the cup in her hands. The silver ring caught the early
light.
 "SubTropolis," she said. "Eventually. When you're ready. But first,
you need to learn to control what you're seeing. Your grandfather
documented his entire process, exercises, practices. It took him
months."
 "Months."
 "Months." She looked at him. In the gray dawn, her green eyes were
dark, almost black. "You're not alone in this, Blake. I know the
sequences make everything seem distant. Like you're seeing the world
from behind glass. But you're not alone."
 She said it the way she'd said it's going to be all right on the phone. As
if it were fact and not comfort.
 "Thank you," he said.
 "For what?"
 "For the Thai food."
 She laughed again. Quiet this time, almost private.
 "You're welcome," she said.
 And freshly, in months, maybe years. The 3:47 AM silence in
Blake's apartment felt like something other than loneliness.

===== PAGE 65 =====
65
 CHAPTER SIX
 THE INVESTIGATION
 109 Hz · Kansas City
Genessee Street smelled like river water and old brick.
 October morning. Sharp. Bright. The kind of Kansas City day
where the light hit the limestone bluffs and turned them the color of old
bone. Blake could see further than he'd ever seen. The skyline
downtown, the grain elevators on the far bank, the mathematical spacing
of the railroad bridges.
 Nadia led him through the West Bottoms at 9 AM, their breath
visible. She walked a half-step ahead, confident, familiar, and Blake
tracked her rhythm without deciding to.


===== PAGE 66 =====
MASTERS X
66
 1647 Genessee Street looked abandoned. Graffiti on the loading
dock. Windows covered with plywood. A faded sign: RIVERWARDS
COLD STORAGE CO. 1923.
 But Blake could see the security now. Cameras disguised as broken
light fixtures. Motion sensors in the graffiti patterns. A door that looked
rusted shut but had fresh oil on its hinges. The geometric placement
creating overlapping coverage zones.
 "Hidden in plain sight," he said.
 Nadia glanced back. Almost smiled. "Your grandfather taught you
well."
 The interior was nothing like the exterior. Clean. Modern. Banks of
computers along one wall, bookshelves along another filled with
volumes that predated mass production. A conference table covered
with maps and documents in Latin, Greek, Arabic.
 And standing at the table, a man Blake hadn't expected to see.
 "Andrew?"
 Andrew Chen looked up from his laptop. Same angular face Blake
had known since eighth grade, same analytical intensity. But there was
something new, a wariness Blake hadn't seen before.
 "Hey." Andrew closed the laptop. "So. This is real."
 Blake looked at Nadia. "You contacted him."
 "Two months ago. When the safety deposit box timeline started
accelerating. We needed a technical analyst. Andrew was already in your
orbit, already had the skills." She met Blake's stare evenly. "He's been
read in on the basics. He didn't believe any of it until this morning."
 Andrew ran a hand through his hair. "She showed up at my
apartment at 6 AM with seven leather notebooks. I thought she was


===== PAGE 67 =====
HOLLOWAY
67
insane." He paused. "Then I started reading Notebook Three."
 "The cross-references," Blake said.
 "The cross-references." Andrew's voice had gone quiet. "Between
cave carvings and classified aerospace research. Blake, your grandfather
was reverse-engineering antenna designs from petroglyphs. Those aren't
diagrams, they're technical specifications."
 "He found something," Nadia said simply.
 Andrew studied Blake's face. The bruise, the butterfly bandages, the
eyes that were probably still too bright.
 "You look different," Andrew said.
 "I am different."
 "Show me."
 Blake pointed at the wall behind him. "The brickwork. You see a
wall. I see a tessellation. Hexagonal stress patterns from the original
construction, each brick positioned according to load-bearing
mathematics. The mortar lines create a grid that deviates because the
building settled unevenly, probably due to the river table." He paused.
"Your heartbeat just accelerated."
 Andrew was silent for a long moment. Then: "Jesus Christ, Blake."
 "I know."
 Andrew looked at Nadia. "Can you do this?"
 "No." The word came out flat. Final. Blake heard something
underneath. Years of trying. "I understand them intellectually. But I can't
perceive them the way Blake does. The Masters bloodline carries a
neural framework that's uniquely responsive."


===== PAGE 68 =====
MASTERS X
68
 Blake watched the way she kept her voice clinical, her posture
professional. But her eyes had gone somewhere private and painful
before coming back.
 She wanted to see what he saw. Had probably wanted it for years.
And couldn't.
They worked all morning.
 Andrew scanned the notebooks page by page. Blake walked him
through William's discoveries. Nadia translated Latin marginalia,
cross-referencing citations against the Keepers' archive. She sat close
enough that their elbows occasionally touched, and each time, neither
moved away.
 Andrew noticed. Blake caught him noticing.
 "Father Moreau," Nadia said, pulling a photocopied newspaper
clipping from the archive. "This is where it starts."
 Kansas City Star, June 6, 1924. Page six. A French-Canadian priest
at Sacred Heart Church had drowned in the Missouri River. Accidental.
Case closed.
 "Page six," Blake said. "For a priest who'd served at Sacred Heart
for two years."
 "Just like your father," Andrew said quietly. "Eighty-six years apart.
Both investigating motifs. Both dead suddenly."
 Blake was looking at Nadia's hands. The way she touched the
century-old newsprint as if it were sacred text. Because to her, it was.
Father Moreau had been one of hers.


===== PAGE 69 =====
HOLLOWAY
69
 "Every time someone starts seeing what you see," Andrew said,
pulling up a timeline on his laptop, "someone else shows up to make
them stop."
 "The Custodians," Blake said.
 "Your grandfather was smart enough to hide. Your father wasn't."
 The room went quiet. Blake felt Nadia's hand brush his arm, not a
grab. A touch. I'm here.
Late afternoon. Andrew gone. Nadia reorganizing documents at the
conference table.
 "You wanted to see them," Blake said.
 Her hands stopped. "What?"
 "The patterns. When you said you can't perceive them, that wasn't
just information. That was grief."
 She didn't answer immediately. "I've spent my entire adult life
studying this. Since Sister Katarina showed me a fragment of the Ars
Notoria when I was sixteen. I felt something. A pull. I thought if I
studied hard enough, I could teach myself to see."
 "It didn't work."
 "The awareness is neurological, not intellectual. You can't study
your way into it any more than you can study your way into perfect
pitch." The saddest smile Blake had seen from her. "So I became the
world's foremost expert on a thing I can never experience."
 Blake walked around the table. Sat next to her.


===== PAGE 70 =====
MASTERS X
70
 "Show me what you see when you look at the notae," he said.
 "I just told you -"
 "Not the patterns. Show me the scholarship. The history. The thing
that made you take vows and cross an ocean and sit in a Kansas City
warehouse waiting for someone like me."
 Nadia's composure broke into surprise. No one had asked her that
before.
 She pulled the untitled book toward them. "Here. This is a figura
notarum. But look at the construction lines." Her finger traced faint
marks. "The artist used a compass and straightedge. Euclidean
construction. In a book that carbon-dates to the eleventh century, in a
tradition your grandfather traced to pre-Columbian North America."
 "Which means -"
 "Which means either the timeline of knowledge transmission is
completely wrong, or there was contact between traditions that history
doesn't account for." Her eyes were bright now, alive with the fierce
intensity of someone speaking about the thing they loved. "That's what I
see, Blake. Not the patterns. The history. The human hands that made
these. The people who carried this knowledge across centuries and
oceans, who encoded it in proportional figures because geometry is the
one language that survives every empire."
 She was leaning toward him. He was leaning toward her.
 "That's what I see," she said quietly. "Is it enough?"
 "It's beautiful," he said. And meant the history. And meant her.
 The moment held. Somewhere in the future, the distance the gift
would create between them was already forming. But not yet. Not today.


===== PAGE 71 =====
HOLLOWAY
71
 Today, Nadia smiled at him. The real smile. And Blake smiled back,
and for a few minutes in a warehouse on Genessee Street, they were just
two people sitting close together over an old book.
They closed the warehouse at sunset. Walking back through the West
Bottoms, they passed a wine bar. A chalkboard listed Missouri wines by
the glass.
 "Missouri was the third-largest wine-producing state before
Prohibition," Nadia said. "The Jesuits planted the first vineyards in the
1820s. They mapped the terroir using angular measurements from the
Ars Notoria. The vine rows followed the same proportional ratios as
their manuscript illuminations."
 "Wine and manuscripts," Blake said.
 "Both ways of storing something alive in a container that outlasts
the maker."
 "Dinner?" he said.
 "I should go back to -"
 "Dinner. Not a briefing. Just. I haven't had dinner with another
person in six months."
 "Thai again?" she said.
 Blake blinked. "What?"
 "Thai food. Or something different?"
 "Something different."
 "There's an Ethiopian place on 39th Street. If you like injera."


===== PAGE 72 =====
MASTERS X
72
 "I have no idea what injera is."
 "Then you'll learn something." She started walking again. "I should
warn you. I eat with my hands. The nuns thought it was barbaric. I
thought it was honest."
 They walked east, toward the lights of the city, the distance between
them closing with each block in a way apart from geometry and
everything to do with the ordinary mathematics of two people choosing
to be near each other.
 The gray sedan followed at a respectful distance.
 Neither of them looked back.


===== PAGE 73 =====

 CHAPTER SEVEN
 THE DEEPER MYSTERY
 109 Hz · Kansas City
Morning Coffee #2 was different.
 Blake knew it was different before he opened his eyes. 3:47 again.
The same internal alarm. But this time, when he got up, his mind was
already in the notebooks. Already tracing connections between the 1882
Wyandotte cave article and the sealed entrance notation on the USGS
geological survey.
 Nadia was awake on the couch. She'd stayed again. The third night
in a row, each time with the same clinical framing (someone should be here
while you're processing), each time with the same unspoken current
underneath.


===== PAGE 74 =====
MASTERS X
74
 "Coffee?" Blake said from the kitchen.
 "Please."
 He made two cups. Set one on the arm of the couch and turned
back toward his laptop before Nadia had finished sitting up, before any
of the small morning rituals that had felt, three days ago, like the
beginning of something.
 "You found something," she said.
 "Kansas City sits on caves. I found an 1882 newspaper article. A
reporter lowered into a well in Wyandotte discovered an underground
river three miles from SubTropolis."
 "Blake -"
 "SubTropolis wasn't built. It was mined. Midwest Precote started
room-and-pillar work in the Bethany Falls limestone in the 1940s, and
Hunt Midwest turned the emptied rooms into an underground business
district twenty years later. But the mining broke into voids nobody
cut. The caves Moreau used were there decades before. Centuries
before."
 "Blake."
 He stopped. Turned. Nadia was sitting on the couch with her coffee
untouched, watching him with concern. Patience. And underneath both,
the beginning of loss.
 "You haven't looked at me since I woke up," she said.
 The sentence landed. She was right. He'd been talking at the laptop,
at the research. He'd been talking past her as you talk past furniture.
 "I'm sorry," he said.
 "Don't be sorry. Just tell me about the caves. But tell me, not the
screen."
 He told her. Slower this time. Watching her face as her scholar's
mind assembled context around each new piece of evidence. When he


===== PAGE 75 =====
HOLLOWAY
75
mentioned the USGS notation, Cave entrance (sealed), historical marker
removed 1967. Her expression sharpened.
 "Removed," she said. "Not relocated."
 "Someone erased it deliberately."
 "The Custodians clean up after themselves." She turned to the
window. The gray sedan was there. "1967 would be right. The
commercial operation was expanding."
 "We need to find Moreau's journal," she said. "A personal journal,
his record of where he hid the manuscripts."
 "I think I know where it is. 1112 Pennsylvania Avenue. Moreau's
last known address."
They took Nadia's car. She drove with quiet competence. Checking
mirrors, varying routes, making two unnecessary turns to confirm the
gray sedan was following.
 Blake sat in the passenger seat watching the morning city. His
perception was sharper than yesterday. Power lines creating interference
patterns against the October sky.
 "You're doing it right now," Nadia said.
 "Doing what?"
 "Going somewhere I can't follow." She said it without accusation.
"Your eyes change when the perception takes over. You're seeing
something different. Something more."
 "Does it bother you?"


===== PAGE 76 =====

 "It scares me. Not the perception itself. How fast it's progressing."

 They ordered at the counter. Blake's coffee habits, inherited from

 The interior was exposed brick and reclaimed wood.
 Except.
 Blake's eyes tracked along the brick wall. A section near the back

 "That wall," he said.

 "It was rebuilt. The rest is original 1889. That section isn't." He

 A soft click. The wall section swung inward, just enough to reveal a

geometric patterns and encrypted text.
 But when Blake relaxed his focus, the symbols resolved.
 Father Vincent Moreau. Personal journal. June 1920. June 1924.
 "Can you read it?" Nadia's voice was tight.
 "Yes. The encryption resolves when I focus on it. Like a lock that
only opens for a specific key."


===== PAGE 77 =====
HOLLOWAY

 "Moreau designed it that way. If you can read it, you're one of us. If

part of the knowledge she'd devoted her life to, and she couldn't read it.

You can read that better than I can."
 It was a small thing. An invitation. Come into this through the door you
can open.
 They read together. Blake translating, Nadia supplying the
framework. The journal documented manuscript locations, access routes
through cave systems Moreau had mapped in detail.
 And a final entry. June 4, 1924. Two days before Moreau drowned.
 I cannot know if I serve truth or if truth has abandoned me to madness. I can
only document what I see and pray that those who come after can determine which I
have become.
 Nadia's hand found his forearm. A grip. As if she were holding

 "We need to go," Blake said. "If I can find this, someone else can

They were halfway to the car when Blake saw the priest.
 Father Crane stood on the corner. Black suit, white collar. He
walked toward them with purpose.
 "Mr. Masters. We need to talk."

 "I think we do." Crane fell into step beside them. His eyes moved

Back at the apartment, they mapped the underground network from

 I'll be here.

 Outside, the gray sedan watched.
 Inside, Nadia's voice held Blake steady while his brain learned to fly.

 CHAPTER EIGHT
 THE KEY
 109 Hz · Kansas City
Blake's mother answered the door in an apron dusted with flour.
 Lorraine Masters, sixty-three. Her face did the thing it always did.
Joy at seeing her son compressed by the worry that accompanied every
unexpected visit. Blake hadn't called ahead. He should have called ahead.
 "Blake." Her eyes cataloged the bruise on his forehead, the butterfly
bandages. "What happened?"
 "I fell in the library. I'm fine."
 "You're not fine. You're wearing the watch."


===== PAGE 82 =====
MASTERS X
82
 Blake looked at his wrist. The Breitling. He'd been wearing it since
Jefferson City without thinking about it.
 "Come in," Lorraine said. "Ethan's here."
 The house smelled like bread and lavender. His mother's house
always smelled like bread and lavender. The same olfactory signature
Blake had carried in his memory since childhood, the smell that meant
safety.
 Ethan was in the kitchen. Blake's nephew, thirty-one, practical, the
kind of person who fixed things with his hands while Blake fixed them
with his mind. Ethan had his father's build and his grandmother's
steadiness.
 "Hey." Ethan looked up from the Sports section. Took in Blake's
face. "Rough week?"
 "You could say that."
 Lorraine set tea on the table. Not coffee. Tea. Blake noticed the
choice. His mother had always served tea when she wanted a real
conversation.
 "Mom," Blake said. "I need to ask you about Dad."
 Lorraine's hands went still on the teapot. Ethan set down the
newspaper.
 "What about him?" Lorraine's voice was careful.
 "The accident. The things he was asking about before he died.
Oscar-01."
 The kitchen went quiet. Outside, a cardinal landed on the feeder.
Red against October gray. Blake tracked the bird's angular trajectory
automatically.


===== PAGE 83 =====
HOLLOWAY
83
 Through the kitchen window he could see the backyard. Smaller
than he remembered, but the beehives were still there. Three white
boxes at the edge of the property line, weathered by fifteen years of
Missouri seasons.
 The remembrance came without warning. Nine years old, standing
next to the hives in a white suit too big for him. James beside him, lifting
the top cover.
 "Watch how they build, Blake." Thousands of bees across the
comb. "Perfect hexagons. Every single time."
 "Why hexagons?"
 "Math. Hexagons use less wax than squares. Hold more honey.
Stronger structure. The bees figured this out millions of years before
humans invented geometry."
 James pointed at the cells. Tilted slightly upward. "Thirteen-degree
angle. Same in every hive, everywhere in the world. They don't measure
it. They just know."
 "They're talking with math?"
 "They're talking with geometry. Pattern as language." James knelt
down. Lifted the veil so they were face to face. "If you see patterns that
scare you. Don't try to figure it out alone. Working alone is how people
get lost. Remember that."
 Blake surfaced from the memory. The bees were gone. The hives
empty now, paint peeling. James was gone. The mathematics remained.
 "Your father was a good man," Lorraine said. "But he couldn't
leave things alone."
 "Neither can I."


===== PAGE 84 =====
MASTERS X
84
 Lorraine studied him. Then she stood, walked to the back
bedroom, and returned with a wooden box Blake had never seen.
Carved walnut, simple, the kind of work his father had done in the
garage on weekends.
 "James left this. In his desk at work. They sent it home with his
things."
 Inside: a key. Different from William's. Smaller, modern, a Post
Office box key. And a note in James's handwriting:
 Don't go alone.
 Three words. The advice of a man who'd gone alone and paid for it.
 "He knew the accident was coming," Blake said.
 "He knew something was wrong." Lorraine's eyes were wet. "He
called me the night before. Said if anything happened, to hide this. Give
it to you when you were ready."
 "When I was ready."
 "He said you'd know when."
 Blake looked at the key. At his mother. At Ethan, who was
watching with an expression that mixed confusion and the beginning of
fear.
 "Blake," Ethan said. "Whatever Dad was into. Whatever you're into.
You need to be careful."
 "I am being careful."
 "You fell in a library and you're wearing Grandpa's watch and you
look like you haven't slept in three days." Ethan's pragmatism, slicing
through ambiguity. "That's not careful."
 "I'm not working alone this time. I have people."


===== PAGE 85 =====
HOLLOWAY
85
 "People." Ethan's voice was tight. "I'm tired of going to Masters
funerals, Blake."
 The words sat between them. Blake heard what his nephew meant,
not anger, not even fear. Exhaustion. The exhaustion of the family
member who stayed behind and answered the phone calls and identified
the bodies and held their grandmother together.
 "So am I," Blake said. "That's why I'm doing this differently."
 Ethan studied him for a long moment. Then, softer: "Be careful.
Please."
 Lorraine put her hand over Blake's. Her grip was stronger than it
looked, sixty-three years of holding things together.
 "Your father loved you," she said. "William loved you. Whatever
they were trying to protect you from, they were trying to protect you."
 "I know, Mom."
 "Then don't do what they did." Her eyes were fierce. "Don't
disappear into it. Don't become so obsessed that you forget to call your
mother. Don't -" Her voice broke. "Don't die on a straight road in
perfect weather."
 Blake placed his arm around her. Held her the way she'd held him
when he was small and afraid and the world was too vast and too bright.
 "I won't," he said.
 And meant it. And hoped it was true.
On the drive home, Blake thought about his father's key.


===== PAGE 86 =====
MASTERS X

 Don't go alone.
 He pulled over. Reached for his phone. Called Nadia.
 She answered on two rings. "Blake."
 "My mother had a key. From my father."

and Blake was only beginning to understand.
 But not alone.
 Not anymore.

 CHAPTER NINE
 THE PREPARATION
 109 Hz · Kansas City
The Analysis Chamber lived in the basement of 1647 Genessee Street.
 Andrew had built it over three weeks. A server rack, six monitors,
pattern-recognition software he'd written himself, and the custom
algorithm derived from William's Notebook Three specifications. The
room smelled like solder and ozone and the particular scent of
electronics running hot.
 "This is what your grandfather was working toward," Andrew said,
pulling up a three-dimensional visualization that made Blake's breath
catch. "He documented the pattern-recognition sequences manually. I
digitized them."


===== PAGE 88 =====

88
 The display showed the geometric figures from all seven notebooks,
arranged in a web of connections. Each figure linked to others through
proportional relationships. Some obvious, some requiring multiple
transformations to reveal. The web pulsed with activity as the algorithm
found new connections in real time.
 "It's alive," Blake said.
 "It's processing. There's a difference." Andrew's half-smile. "But
yeah. It's doing something I didn't expect. The algorithm found
connections between William's figures and known archaeological sites.
Pre-Columbian cave art in Missouri, Oklahoma, Tennessee. The same
proportional system appears at all of them."
 "How many sites?"
 "Forty-seven so far. And counting."
 Nadia leaned over the display. "Sort by date. Oldest first."
 Andrew typed. The map rearranged. The oldest sites clustered in
the Missouri and Mississippi river valleys. Picture Cave, Mud Glyph
Cave, Gottschall Rockshelter. Thousands of years old.
 "The pattern radiates outward from the Missouri confluence,"
Nadia said. "Like ripples from a stone dropped in water. The oldest sites
are closest to SubTropolis."
 "Because SubTropolis sits on top of the source," Blake said.
 They looked at each other across the display. The screen's blue light
made their faces look otherworldly. Two scholars and a pilot staring at a
map of knowledge that predated their civilization.

 "The first exercise is the rosette," Nadia said. "William calls it the

his eyelids.
 "The proportions are the aperture controls," Nadia's voice said.
Steady. Professional. The control tower guiding the pilot through

===== PAGE 93 =====
