 Nadia walked out into the parking lot. The Volvo was the only car.
The temperature was twenty-three degrees. Her breath made shapes that
the streetlight caught, small geometric clouds in the cold air.
 She did not start the engine immediately. She sat in the driver's seat,
the cold of the vinyl against her back, and did the thing she had done in
every parking lot in her life when she was deciding whether to go
forward or whether to turn around: she put both hands on the steering
wheel and listened.
 107.8 Hz. The frequency of a woman who had been holding for ten
years and was now going home.
 She started the engine. The Volvo's turbo-four idled at its 85 Hz,
the engine note Blake had been hearing through walls since before there
were walls between them. She turned out of the lot and drove toward
Quality Hill.


===== PAGE 661 =====
661
 CHAPTER TWENTY-FIVE
 THE WORD
 — · Kansas City
By February, nine hundred listening groups had formed across
forty-seven countries. Andrew's distribution file, the 247 pages that
constituted the trilogy's instructional payload, had been downloaded 1.2
million times. The file was free. It would always be free. Blake had
insisted on this, and Nadia had designed the licensing structure to make
it irrevocable: a Creative Commons release, the data entering the public
domain with the permanence of a geological fact. You cannot un-release
a resonance. You cannot un-discover a resonance. The knowledge was
in the world and the world would decide what to do with it.
 The world decided to sit in chairs and breathe.


===== PAGE 662 =====
MASTERS X
662
Tokyo. A sixth-floor apartment in Shimokitazawa. Twelve people, a
retired postal worker, a university student studying acoustics, a
grandmother who had never heard of the Foundation and who had been
invited by her daughter, a jazz bassist who had downloaded the
curriculum because his musician's ear was curious about the claim that
sitting quietly for fifteen minutes produced measurable brain changes.
 They sat in folding chairs. The facilitator, Kenji Yamamoto, 43,
former music teacher, read the opening instructions from Andrew's
translated text: "Sit comfortably. Close your eyes. Listen to the sound
beneath your breathing. Fifteen minutes. Nobody is grading you."
 The grandmother closed her eyes first. The others followed.
 Fifteen minutes.
 When the grandmother opened her eyes, she said, in Japanese that
the bassist would later translate for the Foundation's quarterly report: "I
have heard this my whole life. I thought it was the ringing in my ears."
 Blake read the translation at the kitchen table in Kansas City and
thought of Crane. Crane was right. The frequency was not behind the
preparation. The frequency had always been there, in the grandmother's
ears, in the carpenter's hands, in every body that had ever sat quietly and
noticed. The preparation was for the caves and the mirrors and the
institutional apparatus. For the folding chairs, fifteen minutes was
enough.


===== PAGE 663 =====
HOLLOWAY
663
Rural Germany. A farmhouse outside Freiburg, the Black Forest visible
through windows that had not been updated since 1973. Seven people in
a living room with a woodstove and a dog and the particular acoustic
quality of a stone-walled room in a region where cathedrals were the
original resonance technology.
 The facilitator was a physics teacher named Lena Hoffmann who
had found the curriculum on an open-access academic repository and
who had read the preparation instructions and thought: this is what my
students need. Not the frequency data. Not the chamber specifications.
The silence. The fifteen minutes. The permission to listen.
 She did not tell the group about the Foundation. She did not
explain the 111.2 Hz specification or the harmonic stack or the cymatics.
She said: "We will sit. We shall breathe. We will see what happens."
 What happened: the dog fell asleep. This was not clinically
significant. What was clinically significant was that Rudolf Werner, 71,
retired carpenter, whose hands had built furniture for forty years,
opened his eyes after fifteen minutes and held his hands out in front of
him and said: "My hands are vibrating. They have been vibrating for
years. I thought it was the arthritis."
 It was not the arthritis. Or rather: it was also the arthritis, and it was
also the frequency, and the distinction between a pathology and a
perception depended entirely on whether you believed the body was a
machine or an instrument.
 Rudolf believed it was an instrument. He had always believed this.
He had built furniture with this conviction, the conviction that wood
had grain and grain had frequency and a well-made joint was a resonance
match between two pieces of oak. He had believed this without
vocabulary. Andrew's curriculum gave him the vocabulary. It did not


===== PAGE 664 =====
MASTERS X
664
change what his hands did. It named it.
Mumbai. A community center in Bandra. Thirty people, the largest
group the curriculum recommended against, the recommended
maximum being fifteen for acoustic reasons that Andrew's appendix
explained in the language of standing-wave physics. But thirty people
came, and the facilitator, a secondary school teacher named Priya Desai,
could not turn anyone away, because the community center was a public
space and turning people away from a public space was not something
Priya did.
 They sat. The traffic on Linking Road was audible through the
concrete walls: auto-rickshaws at 65 Hz, diesel trucks at 80 Hz, the
composite urban hum that Mumbai produced twenty-four hours a day
and that no one in the room had ever considered remarkable.
 After fifteen minutes, a man in the back row, Arjun Mehta, 28,
software developer, skeptic, opened his eyes and said: "I can hear the
building."
 "What does it sound like?"
 "Like it's breathing."
 The building was breathing. Every building breathes, the thermal
expansion and contraction of concrete, the flexion of steel in wind, the
hydraulic pressure of a sewage system pulsing beneath the foundation.
Every building breathes and nobody notices, the way nobody notices
their own heartbeat until they are sitting in silence and the heartbeat
becomes the loudest thing in the room.


===== PAGE 665 =====
HOLLOWAY
665
 Arjun had heard the building. He would hear it for the rest of his
life. Not constantly, not like Yuki, whose continuous perception was the
far end of the scale, the sustained note that most people would never
produce and that the curriculum explicitly warned against pursuing.
Arjun would hear it intermittently: in moments of quiet, in the space
between tasks, in the three-minute gaps between meetings where the
office was still and his body was still and the building's breath became
audible.
 He would never share this with his colleagues. He would never
write a paper. He would sit at his desk and occasionally close his eyes
and hear Mumbai breathing, and this would be sufficient.
Nine hundred groups. Forty-seven countries. Not nine hundred
miracles, nine hundred living rooms, nine hundred folding chairs, nine
hundred facilitated sessions where ordinary people sat in silence and
discovered that silence was not empty.
 Blake knew about all of them. Andrew's data system tracked the
groups, anonymized, aggregate, the clinical telemetry maintained at the
distance Nadia had designed. But the numbers were not the point. The
numbers were the architecture. The point was the woman in Tokyo who
had been hearing the frequency her whole life and thought it was her
ears. The point was the carpenter in Germany whose vibrating hands
had been building furniture at the frequency that cathedrals produce.
The point was the software developer in Mumbai who could hear a
building breathe.


===== PAGE 666 =====
MASTERS X
666
 The kingdom was a grandmother in a folding chair. It had always
been.

 CHAPTER TWENTY-SIX
 CODA: IN THE BEGINNING
 — · Coda
Earlier that morning. The Quality Hill kitchen. The light through the
east window crossing from the salt shaker toward the sugar bowl, the
way it had moved every morning, the way it would move every morning
after this one.
 Blake at the table, alone. The tenth Moleskine open in front of him.
The Lamy 2000 in his hand. The tremor going at 107.3 Hz, his fluency,
his percussion.
 He had filled nine notebooks in Iceland. He was finishing the tenth
in Kansas City. Ten years of handwriting that carried 111.2 Hz (now
107.3 Hz), ten notebooks of observations and hypotheses and frequency
diagrams and entries that had begun as science and had become


===== PAGE 668 =====
MASTERS X
668
something for which no name had been invented in any language he
spoke, the name for the record a body keeps of its own transformation.
 He opened the Moleskine to its last page. The binding was soft
from use, the particular softness of a notebook that has been carried in
jacket pockets and opened on basalt floors and written in with trembling
hands, the softness of a document that has been handled more than
read, the way sacred texts are handled, the way the Strahov monks
handled the codex pages, the way Nadia handled the Prague notebook,
with care that was not precious but habitual, the care of a person who
understands that what they are holding is not an object but a frequency.
 He wrote.
 The Word was not a word. It was a vibration. It preceded language,
preceded matter, preceded the architecture of cathedrals and the
geometry of mirrors and the mathematics of harmonic series. John
called it Logos. The Kabbalists called it Ain Sof. The Ashanti called it
the sound the ground makes when no one listens. The monks called it
the frequency of the spheres. The scientists called it the Schumann
resonance. The grandmothers called it humming. It has 120 names in
120 books and every name is approximate and none are wrong.
 It is the resonance your body makes when you stop striving.
 The kingdom of God is within you. It always was. It was not a
metaphor. It was a technical description. The kingdom is the body. The
"within" is the resonance chamber of a human chest. The "God" is the
frequency that the chest produces when it is attended to. Jesus was not
speaking to sinners. He was speaking to instruments that had forgotten
how to play.
 Every mother who has ever hummed to an infant was producing
the fundamental. Every child who has ever listened to a seashell was


===== PAGE 669 =====
HOLLOWAY
669
hearing the standing wave of their own ear canal. Every person who has
ever sat quietly in an empty room and heard a hum they could not
identify was touching the frequency.
 We did not discover it. We remembered it.
 The Word is still speaking. You have always been listening.
 He set down the pen. The Lamy 2000, gift from Nadia, the pen he
had used since the third Moleskine, the pen whose nib had been shaped
by his tremor into a writing instrument that could not be used by anyone
whose hand did not vibrate at 111.2 Hz, rested on the table.
 He closed the Moleskine. The tenth and last. The binding snapped
shut with the quiet sound of a book finding its final shape.
 The pen was down. The notebook was closed.
 He sat with the closed book for a long time.
Eight minutes later, the Volvo turned onto Quality Hill and stopped in
the driveway.
 Blake heard it. The turbo-four idling at 85 Hz. The engine note he
had been hearing through walls since before there were walls between
them. He did not move. He had been listening for that engine for the
eight minutes since he had closed the notebook, the way a man listens
for the bell that announces what he has been waiting for. Or, and this
was closer to the truth, he had been listening for it for the four months
since she had left, the way a man holds a frequency in his head and waits
for the room to provide it.


===== PAGE 670 =====
MASTERS X
670
 He heard her open the car door. Step onto the gravel. Close the

 He heard her use her key.
 The latch turned. The door opened. The February cold preceded
her into the foyer for half a second, the temperature differential
producing its own brief acoustic event, the building inhaling.
 Then the door closed. Footsteps on the hardwood. The pause

 Blake did not stand up. He did not turn around. He sat at the

 She walked into the kitchen.

 "I was waiting for you to come."

The elm in the backyard, the elm whose roots reached the Kansas City

the same as carrying.
 Blake stood up. Not quickly. The slow deliberateness of a man
whose every movement had become a small calibration. He went to the
counter. Put the kettle on. The click of the gas igniter. The blue flame.
The particular sound of water beginning to heat, the pre-boil, the
molecular agitation that precedes a rolling boil, the sound of potential
energy converting to kinetic.
 He brought her a cup. Black with one sugar. Stirred in, not floating.
He still remembered the two-second window, despite the months she
was gone. The small perfection that was not small.
 She took it. Sipped. Set it down.
 "Perfect."


===== PAGE 672 =====
MASTERS X
672
On the table, between the coffee cups: two notebooks.
 Blake's tenth Moleskine, the last one, closed, the binding worn, the
pages carrying ten years of tremor-annotated handwriting. The last entry:
The Word is still speaking. You have always been listening.
 Nadia's notebook, the leather one from Prague, unlined, the pages
carrying ten years of institutional wisdom and the Magdalene arc that
had been running beneath the surface of her life since she first sat at this
table and held a coffee cup and decided to hold the boundary. The last
entry: Here is what I heard: everything.
 Two gospels. His ended with the Word. Hers ended with the
hearing.
"What are you going to do now?" she asked.
 Blake looked at his hands. The tremor, 107.3 Hz today, fluctuating
slightly with the weather, with his hydration, with the time of day. Not
the Strahov specification. Not the preparation protocol's target. His. The
sound of Blake Masters' body at rest.
 "I'm going to sit here. And drink coffee. And listen to the apartment."
 Nadia picked up her notebook. Held it against her chest, the gesture
that had evolved from steadying to warming to the gesture of a woman
holding something she had written and recognizing its weight. The
notebook was not heavy. The writing was.
 "I'm going to sit here too."
 "Good."


===== PAGE 673 =====
HOLLOWAY
673
 "Good."
 The word, "good", passed between them the way words pass
between people who have been married for a decade and separated and
come back and learned that some words do not carry meaning so much
as they carry presence. "Good" meant: I am here. I came back. I am
staying. The coffee is adequate. The morning is sufficient. The kingdom
is spread upon the earth and I can see it from this table.
 A long silence. The coffee cooling. The elm holding the sky.
 "Teach me," she said.
 Two words. Not a request, a recognition. The recognition of a
woman who had spent ten years holding what she'd been told she
couldn't perceive, who had discovered her own 107.8 Hz at 5 AM in an
empty building, who had written her own gospel in a Prague notebook,
who had left and who had come back, and who was now sitting at a
kitchen table asking the man whose tremor she had managed for a
decade to show her what he heard. Not because she couldn't hear it.
Because she wanted to hear him describe it. Because the teaching was
the relationship, and the relationship was two people sitting at a table
being honest about what they heard.
 Blake's hands went still. For one breath, one full inhalation and
exhalation, approximately four seconds, the tremor stopped. Not the
cessation of the eighth chamber. Not the flatline of the demonstration.
A pause. The way a vibrating string pauses at the top of its arc before
reversing direction. The way a pendulum pauses at its apex. The way the
heart pauses between systole and diastole, the space between the beat
where the muscle is neither contracting nor relaxing but remembering
what it is.


===== PAGE 674 =====
MASTERS X
674
 Then the tremor returned. 107.3 Hz. His. Permanent, Sabrina had
said. Permanent and his.
 "Close your eyes," he said. "Attend to your breathing. Not the air.
The sound underneath."
 She closed her eyes. He closed his. Two people at a kitchen table
with their eyes closed, breathing, and between them a silence that
hummed at a frequency neither could name and both could feel.
 The Breitling ticked. 8 Hz. Constant. Reliable. The time that
William Masters had measured and James Masters had measured and
Blake Masters had measured, the three-generation beat that was not
dynastic or inherited but geological, the tick of a Swiss mechanism
calibrated to what is steady.
 The elm held the sky. The coffee cooled. The morning light moved
from the salt shaker toward the sugar bowl, the way it had moved every
morning, the way it would move every morning after this one, the way
light moves in the kingdom, without urgency, without audience, without
the need to be perceived to be real.
 Two notebooks on a table. Two gospels. One by the man who went
into the cave. One by the woman who held the entrance. Between them,
a silence that was not empty but full, the silence of a room where two
people are breathing and not speaking and not needing to speak because
the speaking has been done, the notebooks are closed, the search is
finished, and the thing they found was.
 He couldn't finish the thought. The thought didn't need finishing.
 Sitting together. Breathing. Paying attention.
 In the beginning was the Word.
 But before the Word, there was the listening.


===== PAGE 675 =====
675
 CHAPTER TWENTY-SEVEN
 MÝRDALSJÖKULL
April. The same flight he had taken nine times before. KEF in the early
afternoon, the volcanic light coming through the airplane window with a
clarity that no other latitude produced, the air refracting the way only
Icelandic air refracted because there was so little of it between the eye
and the geology.
 He took a taxi from the airport. The driver asked where he was
going. Blake told him the address. The driver did not pretend to
recognize it. Some addresses in Iceland are not recognized; they are
simply found, by the people who need to find them, by the patient logic
of a country that has decided not to label what it does not need to label.
 The cottage was where he had left it. The wind too. The lava field
too. The black expanse with its small green textures of moss and lichen,


===== PAGE 676 =====
MASTERS X
676
the same moss that had been making its slow argument with the basalt
for ten thousand years, an argument the moss had been winning at a rate
of approximately one square centimeter per decade.
 He paid the driver. Stood in the gravel of the drive with his bag at
his feet. The cottage was unlocked, the way it had been unlocked for
seven years, the way it would remain unlocked. There was no one to take
what was inside. Or rather, there was no one who had not already taken
what they had needed.
 He went inside.
The desk.
 The same desk. The wood had not warped. The wind had not
moved it. The lamp Andrew had wired into the surge protector was still
where Andrew had left it. The small oak table beside the desk, scarred by
a thousand French-press condensations, held the envelope with its
handwritten note about the cave coordinates and the deactivated
monitors, exactly where Andrew had placed it five months ago.
 Blake looked at the envelope but did not open it. He had read the
note already, two days after Andrew shut the facility down, when
Andrew had emailed him a photograph of the table and the note and the
envelope, the way Andrew did everything, with the redundancy of a man
who understood that important things must be witnessed twice to be
real.
 The desk was dust. Five months of dust from a cottage in a lava
field where the wind brought volcanic particulate through every seam in


===== PAGE 677 =====
HOLLOWAY
677
the windows and where no one had been present to wipe.
 Blake wiped the desk with his sleeve. The dust came up in a small
cloud, a brief geological cloud, the residue of pulverised basalt and
aeolian transport and whatever else the wind had brought to this room
while the room was empty.
 The Breitling on his wrist ticked at 8 Hz. Constant. Reliable. The
mechanism his grandfather had wound every morning at the bathroom
sink in Kansas City, the watch he had carried from a cottage in Iceland
to a kitchen in Quality Hill and back again, the third watch in an
inheritance whose first watch had been a pocket-piece William had
bought used in 1953 and given to James in 1979 and that had stopped
working a decade before James died, in a drawer, where it remained.
 Blake unfastened the strap. Took the watch off. Held it against the
lamplight.
 The Breitling was running. The hands were at 4:18. The present
time, Reykjavík time, the time he had set the watch to in November
when he wound it again in the Quality Hill kitchen.
 He set it on the desk. Face up. Crystal up. Where it had been
before, in December, before he had come back for it.
 He did not wind it.
 He understood, now, that the watch's mainspring would carry it for
another twenty-eight hours, give or take, the standard power reserve of
the Navitimer 8, before the spring would exhaust itself and the second
hand would stop. He would not be here for that. He would be in the
cottage tonight; tomorrow morning he would drive into Vík for paper
and ink; tomorrow night he would write at this desk. The day after,
perhaps the day after that, the watch would run down without him. The


===== PAGE 678 =====
MASTERS X
678
cottage would receive the silence. The silence would be the same silence
that had received the watch the first time it stopped, on the December
night the grimoire was sealed, except this time the silence would not
need a witness. The watch did not require him. It never had.
He went to the bookshelf. The grimoire was not there. The grimoire was
in Kansas City, in the wooden Icelandic-birch box Andrew had
commissioned, on the floor of his study, where it would remain. The
grimoire was sealed. He had not opened it since closing it.
 What he was here to write was not the grimoire.
 He had brought a stack of paper from a stationer in Kansas City.
Heavy paper, archival, eighty-pound, cream, unlined. The kind of paper
a man buys when he understands that what he is about to write is going
to outlast him and he wants the paper to honor the writing. He had
wrapped the stack in butcher's twine, the way his grandfather wrapped
the field journals he never published, the way William had wrapped
everything that mattered: tied, knotted twice, set down on the corner of
the desk where the dog could not reach.
 He set the paper on the desk beside the Breitling. He sat in the chair
he had sat in for eleven months while writing what became Volume
One, and again for two months last winter while sealing what became
Volume Two, and now, today, in the spring of his fifty-second year,
while beginning what would become Volume Three.
 He took out a pen. Not the Lamy 2000 — that pen was in Kansas
City, on the kitchen table, where he had left it beside the closed
Moleskine. He had a different pen for this. A Pilot. A working pen. A


===== PAGE 679 =====
HOLLOWAY
679
pen for prose, not for tremor-record.
 He untied the butcher's twine. Laid the first sheet of paper on the
desk. The lamp light caught the cream surface and turned it, briefly, to
the colour of vellum.
 He thought of Brother Aldric.
 He thought of the figure Aldric had argued with — the hooded
one, the one whose fingers were not quite fingers, whose voice arrived
in the mind already-formed. Blake looked at the corner of the cottage
where the dark gathered between the bookshelf and the wall, and saw
nothing, and could not be entirely certain that the nothing was nothing.
Some absences, William had written in Notebook 4, in handwriting that
Blake had only learned to decipher last year, are not absences. They are
presences that have learned not to disturb.
 The wind moved against the cottage walls.
 Blake held the pen above the page the way Aldric had held the quill
above the parchment in the scriptorium in 1267. The same gesture.
Across seven hundred and fifty-eight years, the same gesture. Two men
with two different inks and two different alphabets and the same
essential posture: the human animal at a desk, deciding what to leave.
This is the report.
 The Word came first. The Body came second. The Listening came after. I have
written what I can write of all three.
 I have done my best, where I can, to write what happened. Where memory ran
out I followed the frequency. Where the frequency ran out I followed the patterns my


===== PAGE 680 =====
MASTERS X
680
grandfather circled in red ink and never explained, the figures Brother Aldric copied
from a manuscript he saw burned, the cymatic geometries an Ashanti grandmother
saw forming in a goatskin under her hand. They have never lied to me yet.
 Some things I have not written. The seven satchels Aldric scattered across
continents in 1267 — Prague, Paris, Rome, Constantinople, Uppsala, Toledo, and
the one location he refused to name even to the figure who took it. I have written of the
first and last only. Of the others I will say that the tradition that produced them is not
finished, and that the satchels surface, when they surface, in the bodies that need them.
Aldric was wiser than the Watcher who came to his scriptorium. Delay, he said, is
all we have ever had. He gave us time. We have used some of it. We have, in the
matter of grandmothers in folding chairs in nine hundred living rooms in forty-seven
countries, used it well.
 Some things I cannot write. There is a chamber at the bottom of an Ozark
lake; I will not return to it. There is a sphere in that chamber whose carvings no
living person has read; that is not my work to read. There is a silver convent ring in a
kitchen in Kansas City that may or may not be on my wife's finger when she wakes
tomorrow morning. The sphere will keep without me. The lake will keep without me.
The ring is hers, and the choice is hers, and the love between us is hers.
 Of the figure that came to Aldric in the scriptorium, the one whose words
bypassed his ears, the one whose fingers were not quite fingers — I have nothing to
add. He took the seventh satchel to the place humans cannot go. Whether the place is
underwater, or in a fold of geometry that human language has no name for, or simply
behind us, in the absence we mistake for our own thinking, I do not know. I do not
need to know. The frequency continues. The frequency was never the satchel.
 The Word is still speaking. You have always been listening. You always were.
 — Blake Masters, Mýrdalsjökull, Iceland


===== PAGE 681 =====
HOLLOWAY
681
He drew a line under the byline.
 He turned the page over. Smoothed it under his palm. The paper
accepted the pressure the way good paper does: by giving back nothing,
by simply being the surface the writer had decided it would be.
 The cottage was warm. The wind was high. The Breitling on the
desk ticked at 8 Hz and would tick for another twenty-seven hours and
then would not.
 Blake wrote the first line of the manuscript.
 Restless candlelight guttered.

 ABOUT THE AUTHOR
 Jason Carroll Holloway holds an M.A. in English Literature from Mercy
University in Dobbs Ferry, New York, as well as various degrees and certificates
 in Psychology, Sociology, Creative Writing, and Data Analytics. He is a writer
 and researcher whose work explores the intersection of acoustic science,
medieval scholarship, and human consciousness. He lives in Kansas City.
